5.3.2
Dimensions of the homogeneous subspaces in the
rotational Hopf algebras

The Hopf algebra
\mathcal{A}
defined in the previous section is graded,

\mathcal{A}=\mathcal{A}_{0}\oplus\mathcal{A}_{1}\oplus\mathcal{A}_{2}\oplus\dots,

where
\mathcal{A}_{m}
is the vector space freely spanned by rotational equivalence classes
of permutations of
n
elements. This grading induces gradings on each of the
Hopf subalgebras
\mathcal{A}^{+},\mathcal{A}^{-},\mathcal{A}^{\pm}
, as well as their
\nu
-specializations.

The problem of computation of the dimensions of the homogeneous subspaces
is complicated, due to the cyclic equivalence relations we impose.
However, certain precise values as well as estimates for the dimensions
can be obtained relatively easily. In particular, the isomorphism
\mathcal{A}\to\mathcal{A}

taking a permutation to its inverse (that is,
reversing orientations of each of the disjoint cycles in permutations)
establishes an isomorphism
\mathcal{A}^{+}\to\mathcal{A}^{-}
, whence, obviously,

\dim~\mathcal{A}^{+}_{m}=\dim~\mathcal{A}^{-}_{m}
,
m=1,2,3,\dots
. Below, we will restrict ourselves
with dimensions
\dim~\mathcal{A}^{+}_{m}
.

Now we will make use of the one-to-one correspondence between partitions
of the set
\{1,\dots,m\}
and positive permutations in
\mathbb{S}_{m}
.
The numbers of set partitions are well known. The generating function for them
can be represented either in the form of a continuous fraction or as an
explicit sum of rational functions

\displaystyle P(x)=

\displaystyle 1+x+2x^{2}+5x^{3}+15x^{4}+52x^{5}+203x^{6}+877x^{7}+4140x^{8}+21147x^{9}+115975x^{10}+\dots=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{1-x-\frac{x^{2}}{1-2x-\frac{2x^{2}}{1-3x-\frac{3x^{2}}{1-4x-\dots}}}}=

\displaystyle 1+\frac{x}{(1-x)}+\frac{x^{2}}{(1-x)(1-2x)}+\frac{x^{3}}{(1-x)(1-2x)(1-3x)}+\frac{x^{4}}{(1-x)(1-2x)(1-3x)(1-4x)}+\dots

For a tuple (not necessarily finite)
\nu=(\nu_{1},\nu_{2},\dots)

of distinct positive integers
\nu_{1}<\nu_{2}<\nu_{3}<\dots
,
the generating series enumerating set partitions with parts of sizes

\nu_{1},\nu_{2},\nu_{3},\dots
only can be given in the form similar
to the last expression above:

P_{\nu}(x)=1+\frac{x^{\nu_{1}}}{(1-\nu_{1}x)}+\frac{x^{\nu_{2}}}{(1-\nu_{1}x)(1-\nu_{2}x)}+\frac{x^{\nu_{3}}}{(1-\nu_{1}x)(1-\nu_{2}x)(1-\nu_{3}x)}+\dots,

which underlines the fact that the Hopf algebra
\mathcal{A}^{+}
coincides with
\mathcal{A}^{+}(\nu)
,
for
\nu={\mathbb{N}}
, the set of positive integers.

The following statement is well-known and can be easily proved.

Theorem 5.5
.

The generating function

A(x)=x+x^{2}+x^{3}+2x^{4}+6x^{5}+21x^{6}+85x^{7}+\dots

enumerating connected positive permutations is the solution of the following
functional equation:

P(x)=1+A(xP(x)).

The generating function
A_{\nu}(x)

enumerating connected positive permutations of profile
\nu

is the solution of the following
functional equation:

P_{\nu}(x)=1+A_{\nu}(xP_{\nu}(x)).

Knowing the number of connected positive permutations of a number
m
of elements,
which is prime, immediately yields the number of generators of degree
m
in
\mathcal{A}^{+}
.
For example, there are
85
connected partitions of
7
elements.
Hence, there are
(85-1)/7+1=13
generators of degree
7
in
\mathcal{A}^{+}
.
Indeed, the group of residues modulo
7
acts on the set of connected
positive permutations
of
7
elements by rotations; this action has a unique fixed point, namely,
the standard cycle of length
7
, while all the other orbits are of length
7
.

5.3.3
The legwise comultiplication

In addition to comultiplication
\mu
, one can consider another coproduct
\mu^{\prime}

of a permutation
\alpha\in\mathbb{S}_{m}
defined as follows:

\mu^{\prime}:\alpha\mapsto\sum_{A\sqcup B=\{1,2,\dots,m\}}\alpha|_{A}\otimes\alpha|_{B},

where the summation is carried over all ordered partitions of the set
of legs
\{1,2,\dots,m\}
. This coproduct, when applied to rotational equivalence
classes of permutations, defines a new Hopf algebra structure on the rotational
algebras; we denote the corresponding Hopf algebras
\mathcal{A}^{\prime},\mathcal{A}^{\prime+}
, and so on.

In spite of the fact that the comultiplication
\mu^{\prime}
differs seriously from
\mu
,
the corresponding Hopf algebras are very similar. As an example, let us reproduce a theorem
from
[
7
]
. Since only a sketch of a proof is given there, we present here a complete proof.

Denote by
\pi^{\prime}:\mathcal{A}^{\prime}\to P(\mathcal{A}^{\prime})
the projection to the
subspace of primitives associated to the comultiplication
\pi^{\prime}
, so that

\pi^{\prime}:\alpha\to\sum_{k=1}^{m}(-1)^{k-1}(k-1)!\sum_{L_{1}\sqcup\dots\sqcup L_{k}=\{1,\dots,m\},L_{i}\neq\emptyset}\alpha|_{L_{1}}\dots\alpha|_{L_{k}},

(4)

for
\alpha\in\mathbb{S}_{m}
; here the second summation is carried
over all partitions of the set of legs of
\alpha

into nonempty subsets.

Theorem 5.6
.

If an arc diagram
B\in\mathcal{A}(2)
contains more than one arc, then
its projections to primitives,
\pi(B)
and
\pi^{\prime}(B)
in the two Hopf algebras

\mathcal{A}
and
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
coincide,
\pi(B)=\pi^{\prime}(B)
.

In other words, for an arc diagram
B
with more than one arc the projection

\pi^{\prime}(B)
is a linear combination of involutions without fixed points,
so that the coefficients of the permutations with fixed points vanish.

Proof.

We need to show that the expression (
4
) for
\pi^{\prime}(B)
contains no summands with cycles of length one for any chord diagram
B
with more than one chord. Pick a chord
a\in V(B)
,
and let
a_{1},a_{2}
be its legs.
It suffices to show that the contribution of all partitions of the legs
L(B)
such that
a_{1}
and
a_{2}
belong to different parts vanishes. Any partition of the set of legs
L(B)
of
B
induces a partition of the set
of legs of the chord diagram

B^{\prime}_{a}=B\setminus\{a\}
, and the set of partitions
of
L(B)
splits into a disjoint union
of subsets corresponding to partitions of
L(B^{\prime}_{a})

into nonempty subsets.
Pick such a partition of

L(B^{\prime}_{a})
and assume that it consists of
k
parts. Then, the corresponding partition of the legs
L(B)
of
B

arises in
one of the following alternative cases:

•

the legs
a_{1}
and
a_{2}
are added to two
different parts of the corresponding partition of
the complementary set of legs
L(B^{\prime}_{a})
;

•

one of the legs
a_{1}
,
a_{2}
is added to a part
of the partition of
L(B^{\prime}_{a})
, while the other one forms
a separate part;

•

each of the legs
a_{1}
,
a_{2}
forms a separate part.

The total number of parts in the corresponding partition
of
L(B)
is, respectively,
k
,
k+1
, and
k+2
.
The sum of the coefficients in (
4
), up to a sign, is then

k(k-1)\cdot(k-1)!-2k\cdot k!+(k+1)!=(k^{2}-k-2k^{2}+k^{2}+k)\cdot(k-1)!=0,

with the exception of the case
k=0
, which arises
if
B
consists of a single chord.

It would be very interesting to know similar theorems
for other part-specific Hopf subalgebras,
for example, for
\mathcal{A}(3)
.

5.3.4
Lie algebras weight systems and the rotational Hopf algebras

In
[
7
,
15
]
, Hopf algebra homomorphisms were associated
to the
\mathfrak{gl}
- and
\mathfrak{so}
-weight systems, respectively.
These homomorphisms are defined by means of a perturbation of
the trace of the standard representations of the Lie algebras.

For the Lie algebra
\mathfrak{gl}(N)
, the trace in the standard representation yields

\alpha\mapsto N^{f(\alpha)-1}
, see
[
11
]
and Sec.
4.2
above.
In particular, in the standard representation, we have
C_{k}=N^{k-1}
.
The substitution
C_{k}=p_{k}N^{k-1}
,
k=1,2,3,\dots
,
is called the
prechromatic substitution
in
[
7
]
.
The coefficient of
N^{0}
in the image of the mapping

\alpha\mapsto N^{c(\alpha)-m}w_{\mathfrak{gl}}(\alpha)|_{C_{k}=p_{k}N^{k-1}},

for
\alpha\in\mathbb{S}_{m}
,
defines a mapping, which we denote by
X_{0}
, from permutations to polynomials in
p_{1},p_{2},\dots
.
The following theorem is proved in
[
7
]
:

Theorem 5.7
.

The mapping
X_{0}
extends to a Hopf algebra homomorphism
X_{0}:\mathcal{A}\to\mathbb{C}[p_{1},p_{2},\dots]

from the rotational Hopf algebra of permutations to the Hopf algebra of polynomials in

p_{1},p_{2},\dots
.

Note that
X_{0}
is a filtered rather than graded Hopf algebra homomorphism.
Indeed, it takes a permutation of
m
elements to a polynomial of
weighted degree
\leq m
, where the weight of a variable
p_{i}
is set to be
i-2
.

Of course, the restrictions of
X_{0}
to the rotational Hopf subalgebras
\mathcal{A}^{+},\mathcal{C},\dots

of
\mathcal{A}
also are Hopf algebra homomorphisms.

Example 5.1
.

Cyclic permutations in
\mathbb{S}_{m}
are primitive elements in the space
\mathcal{A}_{m}
.
The mapping
X_{0}
takes a cyclic permutation
\alpha
with the number of
accents
a(\alpha)=m-k
to the following linear combination of variables
p_{i}
:

X_{0}:\alpha\mapsto p_{m}-{k-1\choose 1}p_{m-1}+{m-1\choose 2}p_{m-2}-\dots+(-1)^{k-1}{k-1\choose k-1}p_{m-k+1}.

The substitution
p_{1}=p_{2}=\dots=x
makes
X_{0}
into the chromatic polynomial:

Theorem 5.8
.

[
7
]

For a positive permutation
\alpha
, we have

X_{0}(\alpha)|_{p_{1}=p_{2}=\dots=x}=\chi_{\gamma(\alpha)}(x),

where
\gamma(\alpha)
is the
intersection graph
of
\alpha
,
and
\chi
denotes the chromatic polynomial.

Here by the
intersection graph

\gamma(\alpha)
of a permutation
\alpha

we mean the graph whose set of vertices is
V(\alpha)
, the set of disjoint
cycles in
\alpha
, and two vertices are connected by an edge iff
the two cycles to which they correspond interlace in
\alpha
, that is,
the restriction of
\alpha
to the union of these two cycles is a connected
permutation. For arc diagrams, this definition coincides with the ordinary one.

Note that for a nonpositive permutation, the indicated substitution yields
0
.
For nonpositive long cycles, this assertion follows from the above example.

A similar statement is valid for the universal
\mathfrak{so}
-weight system.
Namely, the trace in the standard representation of the Lie algebra
\mathfrak{so}(N)

yields the substitution (
3
)

C_{k}=\frac{(1+N)^{k}-(1+N)}{N}=P_{k}(N),

for
k
even. Define the value of the mapping
Y

for a generator
\alpha\in\mathbb{S}_{m}
, by the formula

Y:\alpha\mapsto N^{c(\alpha)-m}w_{\mathfrak{so}}(\alpha)|_{C_{k}=p_{k}P_{k}}.

Let
Y_{0}(\alpha)
be the coefficient of
N^{0}
in
Y(\alpha)
.
The following statement is an
\mathfrak{so}
-analogue of Theorem
5.7
:

Theorem 5.9
.

[
15
]

The mapping
Y_{0}
extends to a Hopf algebra homomorphism
Y_{0}:\mathcal{A}^{\pm}\to\mathbb{C}[p_{2},p_{4},\dots]

from the rotational Hopf algebra of monotone permutations to the Hopf algebra of polynomials in

p_{2},p_{4},\dots
.

Of course, the restriction of
Y_{0}
to the Hopf subalgebras
\mathcal{A}^{+},\mathcal{A}^{-},\mathcal{C}
of,
respectively, positive permutations, negative permutations, and chord diagrams
also is a Hopf algebra homomorphism.

Theorem 5.10
.

[
15
]

The substitution
p_{k}=x
,
k=2,4,6,\dots
, makes
Y_{0}
into the chromatic
polynomial of the intersection graph of the monotone permutation
\alpha
,
up to a constant factor, which equals
2^{c_{2}(\alpha)}
, where
c_{2}(\alpha)

is the number of cycles of length
2
in
\alpha
.
